ParlaMint-DK_20181009130347	Thank you for that, President. Now I have the opportunity to put on this platform for the first time. That's nice. It's okay. Bill No. L 6, proposal for a law amending the daily offer law, is basically about us wanting all children in our society to get the best possible start to life and the best possible chances of getting along. Sadly, there are children who are not doing enough today. It is especially children who live in vulnerable residential areas and who are not given the opportunity to enter our good and solid day care centres with skilled educators. We also have institutions where this group of children is lumped together, so unfortunately they do not meet children who have Danish as their primary language, or children who have Danish traditions and standards from home. We in the Social Democratic Party think it is fundamental that we meet each other where we live, where we go to day care, where we go to school and where we work; that we meet across our society. That is why we are also very concerned that our day institutions reflect that picture, and we strongly support the ambition that we do not exceed. may record 30%. from the vulnerable residential areas of a day-care institution. I am also very pleased that this applies not only to our public institutions, but all the way around, so that the picture across the board is that this is a place where children meet cross-country. We should not have day care centres where people are exposed only to the same social problems and where those who speak badly Danish only meet others who do not know the language, and where they may not even meet children, where having parents who go to work is the norm. I think that is a good proposal on which we have agreed. I am also very pleased with the rubber bands that have been introduced in relation to areas where this is already in a good condition and where solid projects have already been set in motion to turn our institutions into mixed institutions, so that the concern that this will close down institutions is met. This exemption option is really important. Of course, this can't be alone. This must be done in the context of housing policy, family teachers and social standards. It is a holistic effort that needs to be made to give these children as good a start as possible to life and as good a chance as possible to succeed in our society. But it is certainly an important step on the way, and that is why the Social Democrats support it.

ParlaMint-DK_20181009133128	Thank you. As has already been mentioned, the draft law we are discussing here in the Parliamentary Chamber is a matter of amending the day-off law. The bill is part of the government's overall action against parallel societies and is intended to ensure that children from vulnerable residential areas come in today's offerings where Danish is spoken and where they also meet peers who know the Danish language and use the Danish language, and that for that matter they can also learn more about both education, culture and development in Danish society. In fact, quite briefly, we think it is important that we take this step. I don't think this step is easy. I clearly recognise that there may be problems in connection with this great step that we are now taking, but I think it is necessary. I think it is necessary because otherwise these children are simply in danger of falling far behind when they start school. We have seen for many years that they find it difficult to conduct themselves in a purely linguistic way, they find it difficult to keep up when they start school, if they do not feel that they are also associating with Danish children and learning the language significantly better than they are today. Then the draft law also provides for a number of derogations, and I have to say that it is precisely because we should also make this work in reality. Anything, too many children have had a life over the past many, many years, where they may have only spoken a foreign language and where they have not met the Danish culture, and that is what we, together with all the other initiatives that lie in the parallel society play, will try to do something about. In conclusion, I would also like to thank the industry for helping to play a major role in the drafting of this bill. Because this is not going to be an easy task. We know that this requires that this task should also be tackled around the municipalities, but I actually see it as the only way to really get something done about this task, which we have actually recognised across parties, perhaps a long time ago. ParlaMint-DK_20181009134757	To put it mildly, it seems contradictory when the government initiates all its legislative proposals, which translate the so-called parallel social agreements, with a statement that it wants a Denmark based on democratic values such as freedom, legal certainty, equality, freeness of speech, tolerance and equality. These are values which the Enhedslisten can absolutely endorse, but we certainly find it difficult to see that the government's proposals should promote the values that are being talked about. On the contrary, we see the proposals as restricting the freedom of many citizens, their legal certainty and their equality. A reduction in the freedom to choose where you want to live, a reduction in the right to decide where you want your children to be offered today, and a reduction in the institutions in which they can be registered. The draft law we are discussing here reduces parents' ability to choose a day care centre in their vicinity if they live in a exposed residential area. This contrasts remarkably with the fact that just 41⁄2 months ago the Folketing adopted a general strengthening of the right of parents to want a particular place in a specific day-care institution. With this proposal, the thousands of parents living in the non-profit housing areas defined as vulnerable will have significantly reduced their ability to choose which institution their child should go in and thus face clear discrimination compared to other parents. The draft law is therefore also, in principle, very problematic and, in addition, it will lead to a number of very specific problems. This will cause problems from the perspective of the child • what is necessarily the most important thing when there are children who are cut off from attending a daycare center where they live. The Ministry describes it quite clearly even in its representation of KL's hearing answer when they write: The bill may also have consequences for a coherent course for the individual child associated with the community. ▪ This will cause problems for parents, who for many will have longer journey times. As the Children and Culture Managers Association says in their hearing answer, a lot of parents understandably want a day-off spot close to their home. I also think BUPL is quite right when they write in their hearing answer that it will cause opposition to parents and that it will counteract the fact that resourceful families settle in a exposed residential area. In short, it would seem completely counterproductive in relation to what was supposed to be one of the main aims of the draft law. It will also cause the fundamental problem that it is becoming more difficult to maintain a large number of institutions, because capacity cannot be exploited and the institutions will thus face a serious risk of being emptied of children and of having to close down. These will be problems that will be so self-enhancing because it will obviously make it harder to attract children whose parents live outside the exposed residential areas. And so it will be more difficult to maintain and recruit educationally trained staff and managers. Of course, for the residential areas concerned, the erosion and closure of the institutions in the neighbourhood will make it less attractive to settle. And for the municipalities concerned, this means that they will face very major problems in planning the development of the day-off area and ensuring that there is the necessary institutional capacity. It is clear that the derogations which have been properly incorporated into the legislative proposal give the municipalities more time to solve the problems created by the new legislation. However, this does not change the fact that this is a proposal which is, in principle, very problematic and which will create major practical problems for children, for parents, for educational staff, for the residential areas concerned and for the municipal administration in a large number of the country's municipalities. I hope that the massive criticism of the proposals has impressed at least some of the parties behind the proposal. When KL reaches the Children's and Culture Managers' Association, when BUPL and the Parents' National Organization and the Children's Council are all concerned about this proposal, it should cause the proposers to seriously consider whether they have not gone completely wrong. The unitary list will vote against the proposal.

We ended up doing that. Until then, parents had sent their children to other primary schools outside this area or in free schools. We ended up sending our kids to this primary school. The primary school became the most sought primary school throughout Copenhagen by all sorts of parents because they had invested such massive resources in it. Because all of a sudden, every parent wanted their kids in this primary school. So that's what we have to do. After all, we must invest massively in the day-care institutions in these vulnerable residential areas, so that all the resourceful families think that there I would like my child to have good standards, super cool playgrounds, tours in the forest, what do I know? There are so many opportunities to make these institutions attractive to all parents, and that is the only right way to go if we also want these residential areas to become more mixed in the long term. So that is my solution, and that is our solution from the point of view of the alternative. We can't support this bill.

Today, unfortunately, some of the children born in certain geographical areas in Denmark from early life are put behind on several parameters, and this should not be the case. When the government presented its ghettoud game, the Danish People's Party chose to engage constructively in a negotiation process, and with the proposal we are discussing today, we have changed some of what we believe is essential to give children a good child life, but also to ensure that the foundation for a life in Denmark as part of the community of which we have a tradition is created. When the agreement was announced, critical voices were quickly out. Some said that now we would learn 1-year-olds about the Constitution, others were talking about punishment. Let me make it clear that what was important for the Danish People's Party was that the children enrolled in the compulsory learning services are given the opportunity to participate in the activities, games and routines that characterise a child's life in the institution. If you do not know about the Danish traditions, which often characterise everyday life in other children's lives, there is an obvious possibility in the institution that children are introduced to it in learning offers. It can be festivals such as Christmas and Easter that are marked in the institution, or birthdays with song and flaglifting. When, in addition to traditions, standards and values are mentioned, it is because it is important to be part of our community. That is why it is a pity that some people, as a mother, would have been very concerned about teaching my children from the very young, about co-responsibility, co-determination, respect for others, regardless of gender, and I am sure that it is well spent in the long run if parents have that approach. It should not be at a high theoretical level, but as a natural part of everyday life and the upbringing of your children. It is regrettable that some people regard this as a proposal that penalises parents. For me, it is rather a hand-out to the parents who are living in a exposed residential area and have not had their child enrolled, perhaps because they are not aware of how much it can contribute to the well-being of the whole family, but primarily improve their child's ability to do well in life. The involvement of parents in the process is quite natural in order to make them understand why it is important to know the language and the country in which they live. My expectation is that they will soon be able to see that it is good and that they will choose to register their child in a full care offer with the many benefits that can be gained from it. The Danish People's Party stressed in the negotiations that the parents who live in the selected areas and who master the tasks for which a mandatory learning offer should otherwise be provided should have the opportunity to have their child at home, and this has also been met. Of course, we believe that this should be done in a sound manner, and this is, of course, also set out in the draft law. However, there has also been a part in the creation and implementation of this proposal, which the Danish People's Party has been less enthusiastic about, and it is that the learning offer must be free of charge. We have asked several questions about this and, despite the answers, we are still not enthusiastic about that part. But this is justified every time by the fact that if you had parental payments, it would be unconstitutional, and then, of course, we have had to accept it, since of course we do not compromise with the Constitution.

With this bill, we want to introduce a mandatory learning offer for 1-year-old children in vulnerable residential areas, thus helping to ensure that these children come into closer contact with other children and thus also improve their language before starting school. Do you think that they can get into the normal day offer where they can experience that pedagogy themselves? Well, then you can see what the bill says. It is quite clear that they should not have the same strengthened educational curricula that we have just introduced for all other children. Special rules must apply to them. And their parents are not entitled to the same participation in the offer. You can sit on the board of parents, but without the right to vote. This is important, and the draft law emphasises this. And again, how small can it be? The 25 hours must then be placed in a way that makes the children get as much learning out of it as possible, even if they are 1 year old. Chairman of the Danish People's Party, Mr. Jeppe Jakobsen, was even out to say that you're not supposed to sleep for dinner when you're in this learning offer, because it's a learning offer. Then I know that the bill says that when they are in this institution, they also meet the other children. But if you have to put these 25 hours exactly in a certain way, these kids don't get into the same rhythm as the other kids. Then the educators will always have to consider whether the children benefit from this compulsory learning offer, which was actually intended, and then they are not part of the daily rhythm that I think will be so good for these children. What I am trying to say with all these things is that I think it would have been very good to follow the intentions of what was the government's starting point, namely that children should be offered today; that the traditions of strong pedagogy that we have had had continued; that the parents had had the same influence as other parents. But then you ended up in a completely different place with this learning offer, and that's why SF isn't in it. Finally, I have to say that it is quite simply wrong when the Social Democrat rapporteur says that this is due to technicalities, that is to say, because of technicalities they ended up here. It ended here because you made a political choice. You wanted an offer that wasn't free. You didn't want to make a free day offer, and that's why you ended up with a learning offer. We tried to fight it, but it ended up with this forced learning offer, to which SF is now clearly rejecting it.

ParlaMint-DK_20181009162451	From Enhedslisten's side, we share the intention behind the bill that foster families should be strengthened to ensure that vulnerable children and young people who cannot live with their biological parents have better opportunities for a safe and good upbringing with close and stable relationships with adults. Unfortunately, this is not always the case today, when the risk of conflict and breakdown in care provision is high. And today more and more children are being placed in family care, which also means that foster families must be equipped to care for children with increasingly varied and complex needs. Of course, it also makes greater demands on the support and training provided by the public authorities to foster families. We are therefore in favour of the proposal to increase the intensity of the support and training programme that foster families receive at the beginning of a placement, and we are pleased that the quality of the basic course which is mandatory for foster families to complete is increased. So, although there are certainly good tactics in the proposal, we also believe that more could be done to increase the quality of the placements. Most noteworthy is the fact that matters relating to the remuneration of foster families are not addressed in the proposal, although it is generally known that wage disputes often create major problems in the area of housing. Uncertainty about pay and conditions for foster families increases the risk of conflict and breakdown with potentially major consequences for a exposed child or young person. We would like the foster families to be employed on contractual terms which ensure a fair wage, the right to a pension, a period of resignation, the development of competence, education and training, far more than is the case today, as well as protection against sudden changes in the working conditions of the foster families. This would help to create peace of mind for the good of the child, the family, and the authorities. In addition, Enhedslisten believes that we should look at whether a ceiling should be imposed on how many children a family must have in care at a time, so that we ensure that there is the necessary time and the necessary surplus for the children who are placed. So, overall, we support the bill, but we lack more initiatives to ensure the necessary quality in foster families. Those were the words from here.

ParlaMint-DK_20181009165624	That did not give rise to any questions. So we say thank you, sir. Carl Holst and goes further, and it is Mrs Stine Brix who is the rapporteur for the Enhedslisten. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20181009165634	That is correct, President, and thank you for the floor. Like almost all parties in the Folketing, the Enhedslisten is part of the agreement that was made in the spring to prevent pro forma marriages entered into in Denmark from being misused for obtaining a residence permit in another EU country. In Denmark we have some very restrictive rules on family reunification combined with the fact that it is so relatively easy and unbureaucratic to enter into a marriage in this country. It is of course no secret that we in the Enhedslisten would like to see the rules on family reunification in Denmark relaxed, but that is not what is on the agenda today. The current draft law is not at all about obtaining a residence permit in Denmark, so it is not about Danish immigration policy. The only aim of the bill is to prevent pro-form marriages entered into in Denmark from being misused to circumvent the residence rules of other EU countries. When such a bill is needed, it is due to the fact that the police have found that the rules to date are being used by organised criminals who take good care to bring EU citizens together with nationals of non-EU countries, only so that a pro-marriage concluded in Denmark can allow third-country nationals to stay in one of the other 27 EU countries. We are aware that a number of municipalities in the Region of Southern Denmark have developed significant wedding tourism over the years and that this tourism is of great importance, especially for the smallest of these municipalities, and that is why it has of course been very important for us to ensure that the legislation we are discussing here today will not put an end to wedding tourism, but only the pro forma marriages which are an abuse of the rules. After the adoption of this bill, marriage in Denmark will continue to be much easier and less bureaucratic than in other EU countries. On the other hand, the small minority of married couples who have previously used a Danish marriage to circumvent rules in other EU countries will probably be caught in the procedure set out in the bill. We have made provision for the group of parties included in the agreement to be kept informed of the development of the processing period and, in the event of a sharp fall in wedding tourism, we will also be aware of it. We expect that in the new procedure the duration of the proceedings will be short and that legitimate wedding tourism can be maintained to a much the same extent as in the past, so that this bill does not affect a number of small and medium-sized municipalities in Southern Denmark. If this proves to be the case, then it is a new situation, and then we must meet in the circle and look at the rules. But with those comments, Enhedslisten can support the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20181009171716	Thank you for your comments. That's my maiden speech as a minister. The bill here implements the EU directive on electronic invoicing in public procurement in Danish law. The bill requires Danish public authorities, central purchasing bodies and contracting entities to receive and process electronic invoices according to the European standard. Danish public authorities have been using e-invoicing for many years, and the bill will therefore not bring about major changes for the public sector in Denmark. For companies, there is no requirement for them to apply the European standard when sending electronic invoices. They can continue to make use of the Danish invoice standard as the bill only requires public authorities. Companies will therefore not have increased costs of the legislative proposal, as they are not subject to conversion to the European standard. However, for companies trading with other European authorities, the application of the European standard would be beneficial, as it would be possible to invoice in the same way throughout the European Union. As has been the case in the public sector, business will have considerable savings to make by increasing the use of electronic invoicing. This is an important part of digitising and thus making the entire procurement and procurement process more efficient, as several stakeholders have pointed out in their consultation responses. The next natural step is to standardise electronic orders and catalogues in order to further streamline the process, which is why the government is giving priority to the development of European standards in this area. With this bill, together with other EU countries, we are taking a major step in developing a better and more technologically up-to-date way of handling e-invoicing. ParlaMint-DK_20181009172035	Thank you. This bill, too, is about the implementation of an EU directive. Trade marks are used to identify the goods or services of an undertaking. And the bill also includes amendments which are not a direct consequence of the directive. The draft law will no longer require the registration of a trade mark to be reproduced graphically, but only in an appropriate manner so that the content of the trade mark right can be clearly and precisely established. And some of the consultation answers question that, because can we be sure that it can be described when we have had to show it graphically so far? Over the years, a large number of rights have come into conflict with each other. These are now being adjusted to reflect the practice of the European Court of Justice. The draft law also allows a right-holder to prevent counterfeit goods from entering the Danish customs territory for commercial purposes. And there is a simplification and harmonisation of the structure behind the application fees, so that in future an application covers, as a starting point, one class of goods or services against previous three classes. In addition to the Trademark Act, the bill also provides for an authorisation in the Gas Safety Act to lay down penalties. Finally, new provisions are introduced on the transfer of fee revenue from the Danish Patent and Trademarks Office to the House of Names in Viborg. It is a slightly mixed bouquet, you might say, but the Social Democrats support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20181009173720	Thank you to the rapporteur. And then the trip to the Minister of Business. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20181009173726	Thank you. Well, now I've got my virginity taken as a minister. Now I stand here and am more routine than I was ten minutes ago, but also with another exciting bill that deals with the trademark law. I would, of course, like to express my thanks for the speeches in the debate and for the support that has been given to the proposal so far. After all, the legislative proposal is primarily the implementation of an EU directive on the approximation of the laws of the Member States relating to trade marks. We are implementing this by means of an amendment law which is textually close to the directive, in order to reduce the risk of interpretation issues. The purpose of the Directive is to modernise the regulation of trade marks and to harmonise national trade mark systems and EU trade mark systems. The draft law, like the directive, also contains business features which are currently covered by the collective mark law. For this reason, the collective mark law is proposed to be repealed as a separate law. As I have said, most of the legislative proposal is an implementation of the directive, but some amendments have also been proposed in the light of the wishes of users of the trade mark system and legislation. The requests for the bill, which is beyond the directive, have been made at a number of user meetings held by the Danish Patent and Trademarks Office during the preparation of the bill, where representatives of professional organisations, the consultancy industry, companies and universities have participated. For example, the rules governing the creation of trade mark rights have been clarified. In addition, the scope of the Danish Patent and Trademarks Office's investigations as part of the application process has changed, and the time for submitting objections has changed before registration of the trade mark, and options are introduced in relation to the Danish Agency's search reports. An important change to the draft law is that it will no longer be a requirement for trade mark registration that the trade mark be reproduced graphically, as several of the rapporteurs have also mentioned. The trade mark may instead be reproduced in an appropriate manner using generally available technology, such as: I'm sorry, sir. Torsten Schack Pedersen said that in the future you can reproduce a sound mark via an audio file and not know that you need to write it down on music notes as you are doing today. And it certainly seems a little more modern that we can do it that way. In order to strengthen trade mark protection and combat counterfeiting more effectively, the draft law also provides that a rightholder will be able to prevent counterfeit goods from entering the Danish customs territory for commercial purposes. So if there are a lot of fake Louis Vuitton bags somewhere in Denmark, we can stop them from being in Denmark. It is also proposed that the structure of application fees be harmonised in relation to the structure of the EU trade mark office. This means that in future an application covers only one category of goods called commodity classes against the previous three. The purpose of this amendment is to prevent the filling of the trade mark register with unnecessarily extensive registrations. Before registering a trademark, the Danish Patent and Trademarks Office shall draw up a search report identifying earlier registered trademarks. The applicant may infringe those earlier registered trade marks by registering a new trade mark. The proposal here provides for the possibility of obtaining a detailed search report for a fee, so that you can get some of what you need instead of having such a general search report. In particular, this possibility will benefit small and medium-sized enterprises, which wish to receive further information on the reason why the trade mark applied for is likely to infringe earlier trade mark rights. The risk of accidental and costly infringement proceedings will thus be reduced and the applicant will have a better basis for seeking assistance from professional advisers. An opportunity to opt out of the search reports will also be introduced. This can result in a faster and more efficient procedure, which has been demanded by some of the professionals who work with ip rights. A similar fixed track system already exists at the EU Trade Mark Office. In the end, there's this, which, among other things, is... Mr. René Gade mentioned the question of the Gas Safety Act, which provides for the possibility of imposing a penalty for infringement of the European Parliament and Council regulation on appliances burning gaseous fuels, as the regulation states. And no, it has little to do with trade mark law, but is there simply because we had to put it in somewhere. Then we need to put this into the law and, by the way, we consider it to be relatively uncontroversial. There is one more thing that has nothing special to do with the trade mark bill as such, and that is that we also transfer some fee revenue from the Patent and Trademarks Board to the House of Names. This is simply because the Patent Complaints Board has moved to Viborg to the House of Names, which is why we would also like the money to come with us. That was the main thrust of the bill. I look forward, of course, to further consideration and hope that there will be a good and constructive debate during the committee work, and also that SF and Enhedslisten will be able to get answers to any questions they need. Of course, we are also available in the Ministry if there is anything we can help with there.

The report stresses that a much larger amount must be set aside than in the past in order to maintain the goal of a global warming increase of only 1.5 degrees. In other words, we can say that the climate action that is being done right now is inadequate and there is a need for more massive action. It is now well-known knowledge that the temperature in the Arctic increases at double speed compared to the rest of the world. This means that the higher temperatures in the Arctic affect the life and everyday life of the 4 million people in the Arctic twice as much as in the rest of the world. This also means that the melting of e.g. The ice sheet in Greenland affects the oceans around the world, which rise around the world. You could say that what is happening in the Arctic is also something that affects the rest of the world. Therefore, it is important that both we in Greenland are involved in doing what we can in order to stop the consequences of climate change, but also that this is done very much by the government. In conclusion, it is very positive that the government is maintaining the Arctic as a priority in the foreign and security policy strategy for the coming years. Inuit Ataqatigiit looks forward to the report on corporate finance and work for sustainable and economic development in the Arctic. We want to take part in this work as Greenlandic MPs as much as possible, because the more we stand together, the easier we can also promote good intentions and turn them into concrete actions. ParlaMint-DK_20181009190020	First of all, I would like to thank the Minister for Foreign Affairs for his statement. It is a thorough review of a number of the initiatives Denmark is part of in the Arctic. The purpose of this statement is to provide information on the progress of concrete work in the Arctic, with particular emphasis on foreign and geopolitical agendas and international cooperation in the Arctic. Similarly, there is no mention of what Denmark intends to do in the form of new initiatives or visions about what Denmark can get out of a future effort in the Arctic. The Kingdom of Denmark's Strategy for the Arctic 2011-2020 focuses both on addressing the challenges and on the potentials of the Arctic. All the while, we hear again and again about Russian military policy actions in the Arctic region. Most recently, information has come to light that Russia is now reopening and arming a base only 1,000 km from Greenland, while at the same time it is reported that Russia is also developing new submarines with missiles so powerful that there are no current means of stopping them. In other words, the mantra of peace, low tension and cooperation, repeated in the statement as an objective already achieved on Greenlandic and Danish initiatives through the Ilulissat Declaration, unfortunately does not look as sustainable as the statement prescribes. There is a factual build-up going on in the Arctic area, which was firmly established with England's recent deployment of soldiers in northern Norway alone with the aim of protecting the western interests of the Arctic. The new build-up takes place both on land, on water and in space, and Greenland is again a focal point in this new cold Arctic war. There is nothing in the statement about the government's attitude to Russia constantly arming itself in its Arctic region. Nor does it say how the government feels about the US upgrading their radar at Thulebase and now itself declaring that the base is directly involved in the operation of the missile shield. There is nothing about China's new Arctic strategy and the plan for an Arctic Silk Road, a project that can lead to huge increases in Chinese investment and cooperation in the Arctic. The EU's Arctic policy and approach is a rights-based approach. This should be the main tool for integrating support for indigenous peoples into the EU's implementation of the UN 2030 Sustainable Development Agenda. Where are the references to Denmark and the EU's different positions on whaling and trade in seal skins? And what initiatives are being taken to use this issue actively in connection with the Arctic Council and the European Union's observer status? The EU itself has concluded that the EU is underinvesting in the Arctic. What is the government doing to ensure that future EU investment goes to Greenland? The Kingdom is covered by the Biodiversity Convention and the work of the Arctic Council on monitoring biodiversity supports the work of the CBD. The OSPAR Convention, which includes the North-East Atlantic, is currently working on the protection of marine waters. In this context, Greenland and Denmark have diverse interests, which are not mentioned in the report, and I am calling for Denmark's arguments not to follow up Greenland's interests in this matter. The report praises the new digital mapping of the Greenlandic land area, but fails to mention the disastrous charting of the sea caused by the government's relocation of jobs. A new strategy for the entire Arctic Council is on the way, and I am curious to see whether it also includes a defence of whaling and seal capture and a more active response to the pollution in the Arctic which has recently led to a warning now in East Greenland against eating specific whales. It also shows that there are studies on industrial development and investment opportunities in the Arctic, and this can only be welcomed. The first Danish container ship has recently sailed through the North-East Passage, which offers future opportunities for Danish maritime transport in the Arctic. The Arctic is developing rapidly in the economic, transport and military fields. All the great powers are in the process of placing themselves in new positions in relation to this development. With its geography, the Kingdom has a responsibility and an interest not to be denied by developments in the Arctic. The climate will change, world traffic will be changed and the security situation will look quite different than it is today. It is important that the members of the Danish Parliament understand how extensive the changes in the Arctic are and what game Denmark through Greenland has become a part of. In this context, it would have been desirable to have a more visionary and analytical presentation for Parliament's debate on the Arctic. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20181009190720	First of all, I would like to thank the Foreign Minister for his statement and the opportunity to talk about Arctic relations. After all, we know that the Arctic is developing as one of the geopolitical hotspots in the world, as the ice melts and both sea and rural areas become more accessible. This creates great interest not only among the countries bordering the Arctic but also in other countries that see opportunities in new transport patterns and the exploitation of natural resources. The people must switch from a life on the outskirts of the world and move into a life that can develop into a political melting pot. We have recently seen that Denmark, in agreement with the United States, took an active part in the airport projects in order to prevent Chinese involvement and dominance in this field. So it is not just about working groups and climate, but also about a geopolitical race for influence in the region. It is foreign and security policy that Greenland and the Faroe Islands will naturally relate to. It is our existence that is affected by it. That is why it may also seem a little bit taken over when it is stressed at the beginning of the report that relations between Denmark and Greenland and the Faroe Islands will be dealt with in another forum who will say: we will not interfere in this debate. But that cannot be avoided. The geopolitical interest among other nations and actors thus creates new opportunities and threats in relation to the existence of the Arctic. This is, of course, positive when more people want to participate in research of different kinds. However, it is not enough to put money into projects of a scientific nature or to strengthen the interests of individual third countries and organisations. For those of us who live up north, Arctic politics is primarily about the people who live in the area and their welfare. They are greatly affected by major climatic changes, which have a major impact on the interaction between man and nature. The withdrawal of the ice can be followed almost by the naked eye, but what happens below sea level is not so visible. But climate change also affects the temperature of the sea, current conditions and the flow of cold water from the Arctic and south and cold water north. We do not yet know how much it can affect life in the sea on our edges, but if it has negative consequences, then it is serious for the Faroe Islands, Greenland and other parts of the Arctic, where life in the sea is very much the basis of life for the population. That is why pollution of the seas and the warming of the planet are a threat that must be addressed. And not only do we do that, but we do it together with those countries that have large CO2 We need a joint effort, so that all over the world we direct towards a more sustainable relationship with nature. And more sustainable use of the world's resources. This leads me to the objectives of the world, which are mentioned a few times in the report. I think it would be quite natural in the future to calibrate efforts in the Arctic in relation to the global goals, because it is precisely about finding the necessary sustainable balance between people and nature, which can give our descendants roughly the same opportunities for a good life on this planet as we have ourselves. If we can get the actors to lift up together and measure our results so that we can see if there really is a momentum, then we can also avoid these more airy ideas in the debate on the Arctic. It would be a good starting point to identify the priorities of the people of the Arctic, because we cannot relate to them all at the same time. Nor will we get anywhere with this global target agenda in the Arctic unless the Arctic countries themselves lead the efforts. The global objectives of climate, education, the sustainable development of business and the sea are very relevant. The blue economy or Blue Growth is just ahead, and here we have more development opportunities and improvement potentials. Shipping and cruise activity are two of the kinds that require great vigilance, so it is done in controlled forms that also benefit the Arctic areas. As there may be massive sizes of ships and the number of passengers on board, for safety reasons a model for a rescue service must also be developed which can measure traffic. I do not think that the relatively sparsely populated area of the Arctic is able to pay for this alone, but why should they also, when it is mostly about transit or tourist traffic, which has its registered office elsewhere than in the Arctic? Some form of charge could be envisaged in relation to distance travelled in Arctic waters, which are part of the financing of surveillance and rescue services. The system is known from aviation, where everyone pays an aviation tax, which also contributes to the rescue service. But the greatest potentials in the blue economy lie in fishing and farming and products that are developed against this background. 700 million people go to bed hungry every day, and if the world's population grows by another two billion people within the next thirty years, a lot of food will have to be produced. It will be difficult to continue the growth of food production on land, but the oceans are large and cover 70% of the globe and deliver a relatively small part of the food to the world's population. We can do something about this in the North Atlantic, so that we both create a sustainable welfare basis for the people of the Arctic and help supply the world with food. Of course, we must constantly adapt our use of resources so that we get an optimal benefit from, among other things, fisheries resources over time. But we can also develop more products so that none of the biomass is lost. The West Nordic cooperation has shown by examples how marine products such as seal skins, fish skins and seaweed can be used in the fashion industry, which is desperately looking for ways to reduce their high levels of pollution. At the experimental stage the project has been a huge success, and now it remains to develop the market economy. In the Faroe Islands, the development of sustainable salmon farming is at the forefront, which is very much related to both sustainability and global goals. At the same time, we are engaged in sprouting seaweed farming, which can, in the long term, develop into regular seaweed forests that can both reduce CO2 Let us see more business breaks that serve the Arctic area and the Arctic people. But remember, it is all useless if markets like the United States and the European Union keep out the products produced in the Arctic. This was done after the seal campaigns many years ago, and the result is that the amount of seals has grown tremendously by several million and each consumes 10 kilos of fish a day. This means that the Arctic population both lost its seal catch, but also that seals keep fish stocks at a significantly lower level than before. It costs money for the Arctic population and provides a smaller supply of food for the world's population. But it probably gives more voices among those who have grown up in a city and have no other relationship to meat than what you get from a freezer disc in a supermarket. For many years, Faroese and Faroese professions have worked in the Arctic region. We have fished on the coasts of Newfoundland, the coasts of Greenland, in the Bering Sea. We have been up at Jan Mayen at Svalbard, and as far as I know, the Faroese fishermen are the ones all over the world who have fished farther north. We think that a sensible agreement has been reached on the Arctic the other day, but that means that we can only release it to normal commercial fishing in approx. 16 years after a trial period. We have also confirmed agreement with Norway and Iceland on the border crossing of the continental shelf north of the Faroe Islands, which means that our base area is expanded by 27,000 square kilometers, so that it covers a total of 310,000 square kilometers and the northernmost tip is now north of the Arctic Circle. ParlaMint-DK_20181009194233	Thank you for that and thank you for an objective and constructive debate. Fortunately, I believe that the debate has confirmed the broad political support for the Arctic being and should continue to be a clear foreign policy priority. Therefore, the Arctic has also been chosen as one of the five areas of action in the government's foreign and security policy strategy, and the Arctic will also be a priority in the next strategy. The Kingdom's Arctic Strategy focuses on the strategic priorities for the development of the Arctic towards 2020. The vision of the Kingdom is to create the most optimal framework for the development of the region, both in terms of promoting sustainable economic development and protecting the environment, and in terms of understanding and respect for the lives and needs of local populations. However, sustainable economic development in the Arctic also presupposes that the Arctic remains an area of low tension and international cooperation. This was confirmed when, together with Vivian Motzfeldt, on 23 May this year, I hosted the tenth anniversary of the Ilulissat Declaration. Here, the Arctic coastal states reaffirmed their support for the declaration they signed in 2008 that dialogue and cooperation in the Arctic is the best way to solve the challenges of the future. At the same time, the other Arctic states and the representatives of the indigenous peoples were invited to underline their support for the principles of the Declaration. Climate change brings many challenges, but it means, for example: It is therefore very positive that the five coastal states to the Arctic High Sea as well as China, Japan, the EU, South Korea and Iceland signed an agreement on 3 October 2018 to ensure that no fishing takes place in the high seas of the Arctic Ocean until there is sufficient scientific basis for this to be done in a sustainable manner. The agreement is a good example of international cooperation in the Arctic. As another good example of cooperation in the Arctic and the principles of the Ilulissat Declaration, the Kingdom of Denmark and Canada set up a joint working group in May 2018 to look at the outstanding maritime law relations between the Kingdom and Canada. The working group shall, inter alia: Take a closer look at the maritime border crossing for Hans Island. The Arctic Council is and remains the main forum for cooperation in the Arctic. The work of the Arctic Council is aimed at the ministerial meeting in May 2019 under the Finnish Presidency. All three parts of the empire actively participate in the preparations for the ministerial meeting and seek to increase the focus on sustainable economic development in the Arctic. Among other things: We have done so through the organisation of an international high-level conference on the United Nations World Goal in the Arctic on 1 December last year. The Government has also initiated a report on corporate finance in the Arctic which is expected to contribute to the work of the Arctic Council and the Arctic Economic Council. These proactive initiatives are appreciated by the other Arctic states. An important area of action for the Arctic Council and for the Kingdom is the work to improve the communication infrastructure in the Arctic. Greenland holds the co-chair of the Arctic Council Task Force. Improved communication links are the key to addressing many of the challenges facing the Arctic in terms of climate change, environmental impacts, biodiversity, etc., but also to exploiting economic opportunities, among others. maritime routes and increased tourism that occur in these years. Improved communication over the huge distances in the Arctic is crucial for economic development and opens up completely new opportunities in the Arctic. The international interest in the Arctic is great, and it is growing. On the Danish side, we are working to strengthen the involvement of observer states such as China, India, Japan and South Korea in the work of the Arctic Council. The Arctic is therefore on the agenda when I meet with colleagues from these countries who are important partners in relation to research and the environment. As an Arctic coastal state, the Kingdom has a major influence on the development of the Arctic, and it is important that we continue to work to strengthen Arctic cooperation within the Kingdom. The Government therefore took the initiative last year for an Arctic Dialogue meeting to strengthen the regulatory cooperation between Denmark, Greenland and the Faroe Islands. Close and good cooperation is important in order to effectively promote the Kingdom's Arctic priorities in international Arctic cooperation. This year Greenland will host Arctic Dialogue taking place in Nuuk in December. That is what I wanted to conclude the debate with, at least in the first place, and thank you once again for a good and constructive debate.

ParlaMint-DK_20181009195615	Then we are in the field of health, and we shall be delighted with that for the next few hours. This means that too much medicine is being put into circulation, and we know that can be harmful if that medicine goes wrong, for example in our environment. Where we hear it most frequently, both in relation to animals and also in relation to humans, is in relation to antibiotics and the development of resistance. What lies here in the bill is that a system can now be set up so that the large packs can actually be broken and divided into smaller portions, so that once the veterinarian has prescribed a certain dose, it can be delivered in exactly the size of the package. This means that pharmacies must break and maybe like rock and stick in the packs. That is very sensible, and one might ask whether we need to go further than what the draft law here opens up. It would have been very nice if, as a single country, it were possible to require manufacturers to supply the packs of medicines of different sizes so that they could always be supplied with exactly the package that the veterinarian has prescribed. The third element of the bill here is that we need to adjust something about the authority tasks of the Danish Medicines Agency and the economy and the fees associated with the tasks of the Danish Medicines Agency. As I said, it's a mixed country deal. It is right to do so, and the Social Democrats have no doubt that we will support this bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20181009200230	We live in a world where globalisation and the fact that we are moving closer to each other are becoming clearer and clearer every day. The draft law we are discussing today contains a large number of different elements, and that is one of them. a good example of how health and health policy extends far beyond Denmark's borders. With the bill, we need to get past the WHO and the EU, because health and disease do not deal with national borders. Health and disease are both national and international challenges requiring cross-border solutions. This requires cooperation at international level. WHO cooperates worldwide to ensure health worldwide. There is cooperation, among other things. to eradicate the world's great and threatening diseases, where poliovirus is one of many examples. This is not an easy task. This requires, among other things, that: knowledge sharing, research and vaccines, if we are to achieve it. The WHO has agreed that all Member States that continue to work with poliovirus should introduce a biological risk management system and certify the facilities in the country that work with the virus. When the Center for Biosecurity and Biopreparedness is appointed as a national containment authority for poliovirus, we have a very good framework not only to help eradicate poliovirus, but also a framework that can help eradicate other vicious global diseases such as AIDS. measles. As I mentioned at the outset, the draft law also requires us to get past the European Union. Denmark is subject to European rules on medical devices. However, the current rules on medical devices are being replaced by new rules in the regulation on medical devices. This means that the Danish Medicines Agency is faced with a number of tasks for which it is not possible for them to charge fees. The Danish Medicines Agency has among other things: regulatory responsibility for the safety and marketing surveillance of medical devices, the authorisation and control and clinical examinations of medical devices, as well as the designation and control of notified bodies in Denmark. In order to be able to finance the performance of the tasks, the Board collects fees. In order to continue to carry out its work with control and guidance, we now secure with the bill the legal basis for the agency to charge the fees mentioned. The fact that the board can do its job creates security and benefits the industry, patients and users of medical equipment. In the more immediate area, the draft law allows veterinary surgeons and pharmacists to sell medicines to animals in measured quantities so that they do not receive as much medicine as is not needed. And with the new possibilities, we can ensure that less drugs are wasted. Last but not least, the draft law guarantees formal authorisation for the Health Data Protection Agency to pass on information from the Pharmaceutical Administration Register. This is a necessary correction in the law because the register is under the authority of the Danish Health Data Protection Agency. The information from the register shall be communicated to the competent authorities in order to produce relevant statistics in this field. As I said, the bill takes us around the world: from veterinary medicine in Denmark to the fight against polio virus at international level. The draft law is very much a consequence of our international obligations in international cooperation, which are so crucial for ensuring cross-border health and protecting both ourselves and others. The Left can support the bill as it has been put forward.

ParlaMint-DK_20181009201754	Thank you. And thank you for the comments and the basically very positive reception of the bill. If we look at the proposed amendments to the Health Act, which aims to introduce control measures against polio viruses, this is, of course, very much about, as several of the rapporteurs have also said, that after a number of years the WHO has been committed to eradicating polio, also called child paralysis, worldwide, so that we are actually now close to achieving the goal. And the WHO, with the support of others, has. Denmark decided that Member States should set up a risk management system and certify those authorities and companies in the country that would then continue to deal with poliovirus. This is because a release of poliovirus after extinction, where children may no longer be vaccinated against polio, would pose a serious threat to public health. The bill is therefore intended to provide a legal basis for the Center for Biosecurity and Biopreservation to lay down detailed rules for the task of inspecting and certifying polio facilities in Denmark, so that the polio virus is also handled safely in the future, including in Denmark. at the polio laboratory at the SSI. In the longer term, the legal basis must also be used to lay down rules on the control of other dangerous biological substances, for example: as part of a global action against measles. Thank you, too, for the positive reception of the proposed amendments to the pharmaceutical and pharmaceutical legislation, which aim to enable veterinarians and pharmacists in relevant situations to supply measured amounts of medicines to production animals and pets, i.e. to divide up medicines. The aim of the scheme is to minimise the waste of veterinary medicinal products, but easier access to broken-up medicinal products can potentially also help veterinary surgeons to prescribe preparations that are at least at risk of resistance development, rather than choosing a product that comes in the size pack that is now once suitable for the given treatment of the animal. And I fully agree with the rapporteurs who say that the optimal situation would of course be that we had much more smaller packs on the market, and that is also something that not only Denmark has a focus on, but on which we in the European Union have a common focus. The proposal lays down a framework for the fragmentation activities to ensure that the responsible supply of medicinal products for veterinary use is observed and that the safety of medicinal products and animal and public health considerations are also balanced. I would, of course, be happy to respond to the questions that were also specific to this point in the legislative proposal, in order to ensure that the rapporteurs have the necessary information in this area, including the relationship that has existed with the regulation on veterinary medicinal products and the negotiations that have taken place within the European Union. As several of the rapporteurs have also said, the proposal to amend the Law on Medical Devices aims primarily to establish the legal basis for the Danish Medicines Agency to charge fees for the performance of the Danish Medicines Agency's regulatory tasks with the EU rules on medical devices, including the implementation of the regulations on medical devices. And, as several of the rapporteurs have also said, we must also say that it is necessary to ensure that there is sufficient control in this area. This is also about the changes that are proposed that the Danish Medicines Agency may charge fees for the processing by the Danish Agency of applications for export certificates and for new tasks of supervision and control of importers and distributors, so that we also ensure that they comply with the EU rules on medical devices resulting from the regulations. The change of law thus supports the goal of a strong professional authority responsible for medical devices, but also the goal of a strong life science sector in Denmark, and where we just have a focus on patient safety in relation to the control there is. The SF's rapporteur asked a specific question in relation to the Pharmaceutical Statistics Register, where one consultation party, like this one, has used this opportunity to start a discussion about the bill. That is why I just need to say from the platform that the substantive rules are not being changed. After all, the rules provide for the possibility of transmission to other managing authorities for the purpose of compiling statistics for the general planning of health care tasks. And when it's part of this bill, it's simply because the task that was placed back in 2011 in the Danish Agency for Medicinal Products, after the reorganisation that has been done by the agencies, is today in authority within the Danish Health Data Protection Agency, which of course handles data. And that is why it is just an opportunity to say that there is no material change, but when we have the bill open, we also think it is essential, as is making the impact correction, which also relates to who is the data processing authority today. It's not in this area of the Danish Medicines Agency, it's the Danish Health Data Protection Agency. I would, of course, be happy to reply to this in writing to the SF rapporteur, but given the answer to the hearing, I also thought it appropriate to reply to this at first reading. I will, of course, answer all the questions that the committee may have and hope for good committee consideration along the way.

That is why we believe that subsidies are needed. In the long term, we can hope that prices can be reduced to a level where subsidies are not necessary. Then it is great to experience how Danish producers struggle to become Europe's largest; to experience their enthusiasm, their professionalism and see how far the Danish producers have already reached is fantastic. And I look forward to seeing a big export adventure and many good Danish products on the Danish market to great pleasure for Danish patients. I must say to the Enhedslisten that I do not understand the objections raised by Enhedslisten. Nor do I think it is very optimal to import products with pesticides, nor is it for Danish producers, but it is quite different. But what we are doing here is ensuring that Danish patients can get these products until the Danish market is ready with some products without pesticides. And the intention is to do it to the maximum. should last 15 months, so when the Danish producers are ready, you should no longer be able to import products with pesticides. So this sunset clause thing isn't very relevant in this context, where there's an agreement in 15 months. For the gambling thing with the patients: I don't quite understand that either. Does Enhedslisten think that in the United States and Canada, all the many patients who have had these products for many years have gambled? I can't really relate to that. I'll tell you what. I think it's great that we've got Danish cannabis production going and that we can offer Danish patients this solution. And I welcome the change that is now coming here so that we can offer a subsidy. The Liberal Alliance supports this proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20181009210300	Thank you for your comments on the bill. I also sometimes think, when the debate is running, that one might be a little surprised that some people like to talk about that, uh, it's a field where there's a lot of things we don't know. That is why I think it is important, when we talk about experimental schemes, that we also stick to the fact that we are talking about sick people who very much need us to take new paths politically and who do not want to be criminalised just because they like to have a tolerable life and because they experience that medical cannabis gives them the relief that is needed. That is why the experimental scheme for me and the government is also about the dignity, dignity of patients, while at the same time it is about securing new professional knowledge and results that can benefit people who live with their disease. When I say that, it is because in some of the hearings you fall back a little to the same discussion we have had all the way through, where you say that this is quite the wrong way to go. I think it is important that, with the experimental scheme, we open up a new form of treatment, in which we also continuously build up experiences that can support better treatment and that we also provide the framework for it and that we gain knowledge. This bill is then a further development of the experimental system as well as the cultivation system. It contains five major points which should help to optimise the trial scheme even more for the benefit of patients, among others. by providing both a wider range of available cannabis products and by providing patients with supplements to cannabis products. Canada, the United States, which does not meet the same standards as we are setting. Of course it is difficult to move that way, but if it had only been a question of when we have a Danish cannabis cultivation, I understand the desire to just wait. We also want to expand the possibilities of exporting Danish-grown cannabis so that it will be possible to export cannabisbulk, and we also suggest that it should be possible to contract the production of cannabis products. We do so because we believe that it will help strengthen and professionalise the Danish production of medical cannabis and ultimately also help to support the clinical research that will result from the development of medical cannabis, and provide patients with better preparations, which we have more knowledge of, and which can help to benefit. The proposed changes should also help to ensure sensible and flexible framework conditions for Danish processing and production of medical cannabis, both for the Danish experimental scheme and for export. It is important to say that when some of the consultation answers, as Enhedslisten also states, say whether this is not a distortion of competition, then I do not think that this is a distortion of competition, because what we have clearly said on the Danish side all the way through is that a pesticide-free, organically grown medical cannabis is our competitive advantage, also in a global market, because ultimately it is about patient safety, about product selection, about credibility, but it is also about building such a strong production that Danish producers will also be able to manage on the world market. Therefore, it is also my ambition and the ambition of the government to make Denmark a pioneer both in the development and research of medical cannabis for the benefit of patients who do not experience the desired effect of the conventional conventional treatment they receive, and I think that the experimental scheme gives us a unique opportunity to do so. I would also very much like to thank you for the great support that has been given to the experimental scheme and to the Danish People's Party for the fact that we are also expanding with a subsidy scheme, but basically also for the reception that the bill will receive in this Chamber. Because one might say that reception could also have been: why do you already change it now, less than a year after we have started? And there I really think it is positive to see the spirit that has also rested on the negotiations, namely that we know that we are entering new paths. ParlaMint-DK_20181009212042	Today, we are the first to deal with a legislative proposal in which the focal points are patient safety, the legal certainty of health professionals and the continued development of health care, with a professional starting point for ambulance dealers and pharmacists. Both Søren and Søren's mother know everything about the local pharmacist, because it is here that they fetch Søren's asthma medicine and receive additional advice and guidance, and if Søren runs out of asthma medicine and has not received a prescription, the pharmacist can hand over the usual asthma medicine to Søren so that he can continue the necessary medical treatment until his mother gets the prescription renewed. Today, the field of coordination is narrow. That is, for example, the case. insulin for diabetic patients or asthma medication for asthmatics. If we are to hold on to a vibrant, dynamic and modern health care system while at the same time ensuring that we are bonding in the near health care system, it is a good place to start putting the knowledge and professional skills of pharmacists into play. The last part of the bill is about the demands we make on regions, municipalities and private companies that provide health care. It should be safe to be a patient in the Danish health care system. Treatment sites must be organised in such a way that, for example: the right facilities are available so that a caregiver can do his job conscientiously and conscientiously. That point is clarified by the draft law here. The Left can support the bill as put forward.
ParlaMint-DK_20181009212539	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next rapporteur is Mrs Stine Brix, Enhedslisten.
ParlaMint-DK_20181009212546	Thank you. Here again we have a legislative proposal which consists of a number of different independent proposals. Two of the three of us think we're good, so it's such a cliffhanger for the audience. I will take the proposals one at a time and end by proposing that we divide the bill into two. The first part of the proposal is, after all, about the authorisation of ambulance dealers. We'll back that up. We think it is absolutely necessary that the staffing of ambulances should be subject to the duties and rights which come with a medical certificate. This means, of course, that we can ensure that they have a high and uniform professionalism when they become protected titles. Then, of course, there are also a number of obligations to exercise care and conscientiousness, as is also the case with other authorised health professionals. This also means that they are covered by the same rules of complaint, compensation and supervision as we know from the rest of the health services. The second part of the bill concerns the introduction of a new function, namely the treatment of pharmacists, who must be able to re-order certain medicines and prescribe dose dispensation. This is the part we're critical of. I understand the background to this proposal and what you have thought, and I can easily understand that it can be annoying if you have forgotten to renew your prescription. I've been there, too. However, I think we should be very careful that a concern for convenience does not take precedence over patient safety, and I think there is a risk of that here. We know that in the event of an emergency, for example: In relation to insulin, as the Left rapporteur mentioned, or asthma, including the adrenaline pens, you can give the medicine today. At the same time, it is also the case that if you have forgotten to renew your prescription and it is important that you continue with your medicine and that it is not interrupted because you have to wait for your doctor to open again, yes, then you can call a guard doctor. Treating pharmacists must have a training period of 1 to 3 days. I simply do not think this is enough to be able to assess whether a patient is in stable treatment. It sounds very simple when people say that if you've been prescribed a drug before, you can just continue with it. It's not that simple. It is very important that you can assess the patient's clinical condition • whether there is a development in it, whether there has been another, new medicine that may have a contra-indication in relation to the medicine. That is why the general practitioner has a long practical education alongside his university education. More than 6 years of special training need clinical experience precisely in order to assess the patient's condition. If you read the consultation answers that have come from the Danish Society for Clinical Pharmacology, you can also see that they go through a large number of examples • some of those they have listed could be subject to re-ordination • how problematic it can be, i.e. what it is for some clinical assessments you have to be able to make in order to assess whether it makes sense to re-order. That is precisely why you not only have free access to a medicine, but sometimes have to go up and see your doctor. The example of the birth control pill is also mentioned. Today it is the case that the Danish Health Service has said that you have to see your doctor once a year, because we know that there is an increased risk of, among other things. blood clots. If you just get a re-ordination it will always be 3 months, because that's how the pill packs are... we're in danger of getting into conflict with these health-care checks we've put in. We have asked a large number of questions which have been sent out today and which will ask more questions about this part of the bill. The third part of the bill is to clarify the organisational responsibility for errors and for other health care conditions which may endanger patient safety. So that means that, for example, regions, municipalities or companies providing health care must be obliged to organise work in a way that allows health professionals to do their work in a professionally sound manner. This does not, of course, change the fact that the individual health care provider also always has a responsibility for his or her own professionalism. However, this is a reaction to the fact that we see that errors in health care are often about how many resources are available and how work is organised, more than it is necessarily about a single person. I have noticed that the Medical Association does not think that it is strong enough, as formulated in the draft law. I have some doubts as to what exactly is meant. We have asked a question to clarify whether it could be stronger, because I do not see any real disagreement. But let us look at this in the committee work. However, to sum up: two out of three parts we can support and we propose that the bill be divided into two.

Even if this is something completely, completely different, let's try it. So we would like to propose in the committee's work that it was an experimental system with an evaluation. How rigid it should be, I haven't gotten to it. It must be our normal rapporteur who has to do so. But it is a good element that will improve the service. The risk that it will be decisive for some patients is that there is something safety or something irresponsible in it we need to look at. We are not really so nervous about it immediately. However, we are listening to the consultation answers, and that is why we would also like to have an established evaluation included in the legislative proposal. We'll see if we can do that. Otherwise, it could be an opportunity to divide up the proposal, as the Enhedslisten's rapporteur said. However, as a starting point, we are in agreement and positive with all three elements.

We call it a risk-sharing in connection with subsidies for new medicines. This is what we hope will facilitate access to prescription medicines and thus give patients the benefits. The idea is that when you make an assessment of a new medicine and you may find out that you have to give it what is termed a subsidy, it means that you make an assessment of how many patients it safely, possibly, will probably be discharged to the hospital you now have an opportunity to say: we hope and believe that there are 100 patients who need the new product. We are setting it off, but if it turns out that perhaps 500 patients need it, yes, then the cost of public health care will have to cover the 100 patients. However, if there are 500 patients, the pharmaceutical company that produced the medicine will have to cover the cost of the last patients, that is, those above the 100. This means that the uncertainty in terms of whether or not you can control the economy in this area disappears. In any case, we know what the maximum cost will be from the public sector. It's really good, it's really exciting to get this done. But we also have one but from the Socialist side.
