ParlaMint-DK_20181009215637	Thank you to Mrs Liselott Blixt. Welcome to Mrs. Jane Heitmann.
ParlaMint-DK_20181009215649	Denmark is a good country. Denmark has a well-developed healthcare system where skilled doctors and nurses provide treatment every day, both medically and surgically, of high quality. Denmark is also a good country to operate in. Denmark has the life science industry, which is among the world's leaders, and we can be proud of that. We have companies that contribute to the research and development of medicine and medical equipment, companies that make a difference, not only for Danish patients, but for people around the world. Some will think that we can just sit back and look at both the beauty spots that we can spot and the development potentials that life science holds. We in the Left do not think so. Danish patients need a strong life science cluster, and life science needs Danish patients. But how can this be? The fact is that in Denmark we have a well-functioning subsidy system which guarantees Danish patients a fair subsidy, for those who have the highest medical costs are also those who receive the highest subsidies. This is both fair and solidarity. But we all know it: The development roars out there, and often very expensive innovative medicines find their way out to Danish patients. Or do they? Because we have a small challenge with our subsidy system. The Danish Medicines Agency works with two types of grants. A general grant or a general clause. The general supplement is the supplement that most of us know from the general drug purchase at the pharmacy, while the generally excluded supplement can be obtained by a patient when the medicine is prescribed for the treatment of certain diseases or a defined patient group and the doctor at the prescription has indicated that the patient meets the supplement clause. However, there is a risk for the public sector, namely a medicinal product which is subsidised to patients who are not covered by the subsidy clause and where the cost of the medicinal product thus increases for the public sector. This means that the Danish Medicines Agency can refuse a grant, and it is of course not in the interests of the patient, nor in the interests of the manufacturer of the medicinal product. In March 2018, the government presented a growth plan for life science with 36 initiatives, and one of the initiatives in this context was a risk-sharing proposal, i.e. a model in which the pharmaceutical industry assumes financial risk and costs if a given drug is prescribed to patients without a given clause. With the bill here, the government is proposing a three-year trial scheme which includes two medicines for which patients are not automatically supported today. I have listened to the fact that there are more parties who think that it should be extended by more than these two medicines. I am, in effect, pleased with the life science strategy that we have and the financial resources that have been set aside for it. I do not know exactly what it is like with the Social Democrat rapporteur. As I remember, social democracy is not part of the life science strategy, but I am thinking already that we can have an economic challenge that we need to talk about. The medicinal products will be calculated on the basis of an application round and provided that the applicant company concludes a risk-sharing agreement with the public sector. In this way, the company assumes the financial risk of inappropriate first choice, so that the public authorities do not run the risk of having a medical bill which is financially out of control. Such an experimental scheme is beneficial both to the public and to pharmaceutical companies and to patients. So this is a win win win-win situation. Patients receive a supplement for a medicine for which they may not have received a supplement before. The pharmaceutical company obtains advantages on export markets because it often makes a positive difference to sales when the product has obtained a subsidy status in the country of origin. This means, among other things, that products are more likely to obtain subsidies on some export markets. And we ensure that the cost of the grant does not run away if there are more patients than expected to discharge the medication in question. As I have already mentioned, we are in Denmark's strong position when it comes to life science. We have skilled companies, a strong health service and a sovereign research environment. Denmark has the opportunity to be even stronger in the future. In the Left, we look forward to following the experimental scheme, which will be evaluated in 2021, and we look forward to following the development of life science, because there is undoubtedly an untapped potential that we must have unfolded. The Left can support the proposal as it has been put forward.

ParlaMint-DK_20181009220700	Thank you. But we also know in practice that this is really, really difficult. For what we can see in many of the countries where we are trying to do this kind of risk-sharing is quite a fight about whether or not there has actually been an effect, in other words really a legal wrangling between industry and the public authorities to find out how it ended up, and who will pay? That is why I think it is actually sensible to put forward a slightly different model, which is easy to quantify, namely on a fixed number of patients, who have then been assessed as being those who will have an effect. Then we must try to see whether those assessments are actually correct and so on. ParlaMint-DK_20181009221426	Thank you to Mrs. Kirsten Normann Andersen and welcome to Mr. Erik Lund of the Conservative People's Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20181009221435	Thank you. I could almost have done it just as briefly, but I fully agree with Mr. René Gade in that the cooperation that I have just experienced today, on the one hand in the committee and on the other hand in what is going on here, is quite exceptional, and I hope it is also in other places here at Castle. However, on the proposal here, I would like to say that in Denmark today we have a well-functioning subsidy system which ensures that subsidies for medicinal products are granted in proportion to the individual patient's need for eligible medicines. The new proposal will then further strengthen patients' access to prescription medicines and at the same time allow patients to make more rapid and easier use of new and innovative medicines. We can do this by providing for risk-sharing so that public health services can be freed from the economic risk associated with inappropriate first choice. In fact, the proposal makes it possible for medicinal products where there is a financial risk of inappropriate first choice and which, therefore, would not now receive generally agreed subsidies, to be able to do so by deciding on risk-sharing by the Danish Medicines Agency with the consent of the company. After all, the risk sharing means that the public health service covers the cost of grants to a specified number of patients who are assessed to comply with the grant clause, while pharmaceutical companies cover the cost of subsidies to the public sector for the number of patients who exceed what was expected. That is to say, patients get the subsidy they need, but you share this very thing. It's not going to be a fifty-fifty division, but it's going to be where it's going to exceed what you've been expecting. From the conservative point of view, it is quite reasonable for the public health services and pharmaceutical companies to share that risk, which is why we can fully support the overall proposal. Thank you.
ParlaMint-DK_20181009221640	Thank you. Then the trip has come to the Minister for Health, who is warmly welcome on the platform.
ParlaMint-DK_20181009221651	Thank you, President. Thank you for your comments and for the positive reception of the bill. We basically have a well-established and well-functioning medical supplement system in Denmark, which, of course, contributes greatly to ensuring that supplements to medicines are also provided in relation to the individual patient's need for eligible medicines. We are also a country where we are basically good at bringing in new medicines early, but that is not to say that we cannot do better, and therefore also the legislative proposal that we are discussing today. The government wants to further strengthen patients' easy access to prescription medicines, which is why the government also wants to allow patients to experience even more rapid and easier use of new, innovative medicines than we do today. This is also the reason why, with the life science strategy we presented in March, the government is proposing a three-year trial scheme in which the Danish Medicines Agency is given the opportunity to announce a prescription drug in general, conditional grants on terms of risk-sharing, in other words, where a sharing relationship with the pharmaceutical industry is agreed in relation to getting a product out more quickly on the market for the benefit of patients. In addition to the fact that the proposal is expected to benefit patients, it is also expected to benefit the Danish pharmaceutical sector and the companies, the Danish life science sector. The proposal for the introduction of a risk-sharing pilot scheme, as several of the rapporteurs have also said, is just one of the many new initiatives in the Government's Life Science Growth Plan launched in March 2018 and subsequently made a political agreement in the Folketing with the support of the Radical Left and the Danish People's Party. That is why it will also be possible to read from the draft law that the 1.5 million. kr., which is used for administration at the Danish Medicines Agency to manage a risk-sharing system, comes from the growth plan on life science, which was concluded with the Radical Left and the Danish People's Party. The proposal, after all, by means of the legislative proposal that we are debating today, allows for the possibility that, in future, subsidies for medicinal products which today do not meet the criteria for this can be obtained in general. That is why, of course, there is also an economy associated with it, as well as there is a risk associated with it. In Denmark we do not currently have experience of risk-sharing in the pharmaceutical subsidy system, but there is no doubt that we in the government hope that the experimental scheme will allow us to gain that experience. Therefore, a relatively short trial period has also been planned, which will also have to be evaluated before the end of 2021, and which is also proposed in the draft law, there is also the possibility of being able to extend the experimental scheme on a legal basis, depending on how we see it going. I also think it is important to say that this is a new area that we are entering, where it is also important that we have some experience, both on behalf of patients, but also in relation to the interaction with industry, when we are now launching a new grip on our pharmaceutical subsidy system, which is risk-sharing. Of course, I am making myself available for any questions that may be raised during the committee procedure, and I am also pleased with the positive support that the draft law has received.

Normally we're not that big a follower of punishment... it's supposed to be the preventive thing we're going in and strengthening... but in this area, we're positive about increasing the amount of fines. Then you could go in there and look at where it is you have to start with this control effort, for example. around schools where there are kiosks and other places where you can buy cigarettes. If there are limited resources and there is always a need to start places where there is a whole natural flow of children and young people. Otherwise, we should just welcome the fact that in committee we are considering the fact that, as some of the consultative parties have said, we might be able to include such a completely sealed ID check during a start-up period. It's not in the bill right now. But I find it difficult to see what would prevent the fact that it was actually required for a period of time to ask for ID when buying cigarettes. Because otherwise it's almost impossible. We can do enough from in here, but it's hard to believe that it's gonna be a real blow unless you introduce it. It is a bit of a hassle, it will also be for businessmen, but we would certainly like to include it in the committee's proceedings. Again, we are very much in favour of the legislative proposal, and we can also count on our support.
ParlaMint-DK_20181009225131	Thank you, sir. René Gade and welcome to Mrs Kirsten Normann Andersen from the Socialist People's Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20181009225140	Thank you. In SF we are very concerned about the culture of alcohol and tobacco among Danish young people. Many young people under the age of 18 drink more than they should, have relatively easy to buy alcohol in stores, which they are not old enough to buy, and then the alcohol onset of young people falls on average at a very early age. That is why, in the SF, we also want better enforcement of age limits for the sale of alcohol and tobacco to young people under 18 years of age. The SF wants to protect young people from starting to smoke and therefore from the harmful consequences of smoking and alcohol. Therefore, the SF also supports the proposal that the amount of the fine should be increased in the event of a breach of the law. Greater sanctions for infringement of the age limit will hopefully lead dealers to comply with the law, which will make it more difficult for young people to buy alcohol and tobacco. In this way, we can hopefully contribute to the objective of a smoke-free generation 2030. In relation to the bill, SF does not directly support the fact that the supervision of negotiating points or the supervision of compliance with the prohibition on the sale of alcohol and tobacco to young people under 16 and 18 years of age are moved from the Danish Medicines Agency to the Danish Security Agency in Esbjerg. It is important for SF that the control of medicinal products is carried out by the board with the greatest expertise in the field, and we believe that the Danish Medicines Agency does. That is why we also believe that the Danish Medicines Agency should maintain supervision, as a division of tasks within the two agencies can lead to confusion and discommunication, and we think this is unnecessary. In order for it to make sense to divide the tasks into the Danish Medicines Agency and the Danish Safety Authority, it will require both agencies to have comprehensive pharmaceutical knowledge, which will require further training in both agencies, and why not just retain the task at the Danish Medicines Agency. In order to ensure the best possible control in relation to the sale of alcohol and tobacco and the largest medical knowledge, we therefore recommend that the Danish Medicines Agency should continue to carry out the task of supervision, with the possibility of further sanctions in breach of the law on the age limit, and therefore we do not simply support the proposal to move the supervisory task from the Danish Medicines Agency to the Danish Agency for Safety and Health at Work. The point is, in principle, the same as that expressed by the Social Democratic Party, namely that supervision is a health issue. I therefore hope that there will also be room for further discussions on further strengthening supervision until the third reading of the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20181009225659	Thank you, sir. Erik Lund, and welcome to the Minister of Health.
ParlaMint-DK_20181009225709	Thank you for your comments. The aim of the government is to achieve a better balance between Denmark and Denmark. This also means that the jobs of the state must be widely distributed in Denmark and that we must benefit from the competences and professional environments that exist throughout the country. There was the background for better balance in which we relocated around 4,000 government jobs to the whole country. This is the reason for better balance sheet II, where we are again taking the initiative to relocate in the order of 4,000 government jobs to the whole country. As part of Better Balance II, the government wishes to bring together a number of product safety regulation and oversight services, which are currently spread across several institutions by ministries, in one market surveillance centre at the Danish Security Agency in Esbjerg. It is essential, when we talk about Denmark in a better balance, that we not only talk about it, but that we also act on the basis of it, so that we bring together the supervision precisely so that we can ensure a quality, coordination across supervisory tasks, just as in the end we will also reduce the burdens on the shops that are supervised, because we have coordinated supervision, where we also use resources together and reach longer. As part of Better Balance II, the government also wants to strengthen enforcement of the rules on age limits in stores. This means that we are providing additional competence for the enforcement that is currently exclusively with the police, and the additional enforcement competence we are also placing on the Security Board in Esbjerg. We just have to note that the enforcement of the rules on age limits has simply not been strong enough. Several of the rapporteurs have also pointed out that, if you did not know in advance, the series of articles published by the Politiken revealed it, at least to a large extent. Too many young people have too easy to buy tobacco, and one can say that it could be because the legislation was unclear, but it is not. Danish legislation is very clear and clear: tobacco cannot be sold to children and young people under 18 years of age. I also hear that we all agree in the House that we must make it easy for young people to opt out of cigarettes and alcohol, and the more ways we can help them along the way, the better. It is about control, but it is also very much about culture and cultural change, both in the community of young people, but also in the shops in relation to the sale of tobacco and asking for identification when someone wants to buy tobacco. It is the sole responsibility of dealers not to sell tobacco and alcohol to minors. Danish legislation is very clear. That is why I am also pleased that the bill strengthens the control of age limits in shops, and with the bill we are also sending a strong signal to shops and dealers that it is not a free round to violate the ban on selling tobacco and alcohol to minors. They can risk both control and now stricter enforcement, including possible anonymous controls and also large fines if they are caught selling to children and young people under the age of 18. There is also a link between the bill we are discussing today and the bill on the track and trace system for the whole of the tobacco sector, which the government is putting forward a little later at the meeting, where we are also calling for a further tightening of sanctions, in relation to the fact that, in addition to the three infringements, the Security Agency can also, in the event of repeated infringements, impose a quarantine and thus a sales ban on the retail shops involved. Of course, I am making myself available for any questions that may be in the committee, but I hope that there will also be support for the part that is about ensuring a better balance for Denmark, where this bill is part of ensuring that state jobs are concentrated and assembled in several places in the country outside the metropolitan area.
